```
Fixpoint subst (sigma : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => sigma x
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (subst sigma t1) (subst sigma t2)

  end.
```

### Definitions

```coq
Definition sn (t : tm) : Prop := Acc step t.

Definition subst (sigma : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm := ...

Definition cons (x : atom) (v : tm) (sigma : atom -> tm) : atom -> tm :=
  fun y => if Nat.eqb x y then v else sigma y.
```

LR:
```coq
Fixpoint rel (T : ty) : tm -> Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => fun t => sn t
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => fun t => sn t /\ forall v, value v -> rel T1 v -> rel T2 (tm_app t v)
  end.
```

Hmm, for the base case Bool, using `sn t` is fine for SN but we need to know something to prove the if-case! For the fundamental theorem at `T_If`: `t1` has type Bool, so `rel Bool (subst sigma t1) = sn (subst sigma t1)`, same for t2, t3 with type T. We need `sn (if t1' then t2' else t3')` where t1' = subst sigma t1, etc. Given `sn t1'`, `sn t2'`, `sn t3'`, can we prove `sn (if t1' then t2' else t3')`? In CBV, the `if` reduces: if t1' is true → t2', if false → t3', or if t1' steps → if t1'' ... So `if` is SN iff t1' is SN, t2', t3' are SN. Standard trick: prove a generalized lemma by well-founded induction on the *pair* / using nested Acc induction:

`Lemma sn_if : forall a, sn a -> forall b c, sn b -> sn c -> sn (if a b c)`.

Proof: induction on `sn a` (Acc induction gives IH: forall a', step a a' -> (forall b c, sn b -> sn c -> sn (if a' b c))). Wait! Acc induction gives us `IH : forall y, step a y -> P y` where `P y := forall b c, sn b -> sn c -> sn (if y b c)` if we state the lemma with the `forall b c` after the Acc hypothesis. So:

```
Lemma sn_if : forall a, sn a -> forall b c, sn b -> sn c -> sn (tm_if a b c).
Proof.
  intros a Ha. induction Ha as [a Hstep IH].  (* Acc_ind *)
  intros b c Hb Hc.
  apply Acc_intro. intros t' Ht'.
  inversion Ht' as [...]; subst.  (* cases: iftrue, iffalse, if-step *)
  - exact Hb.   (* ST_IfTrue *)
  - exact Hc.
  - (* ST_If: a --> a' *) apply IH; assumption.
Qed.
```

Yes! That's the right way. Because `P` includes the `forall b c` part.

```
Acc_ind : forall (R : X -> X -> Prop) (P : X -> Prop),

```

**Measure approach.** We want: for all well-typed (closed) t, SN t. Actually, we can prove something stronger by induction on the typing derivation with a suitable measure.

Actually, here's a clean classic measure (I recall this from a course): For STLC, define for each term t a natural number `‖t‖` by:
```
‖x‖ = 1
‖λx:A. t‖ = ‖t‖ + 1
‖t1 t2‖ = ‖t1‖ + ‖t2‖ + (something depending on the type of t1 and ‖t2‖)
```
The trick: use the type to bound how much the size can grow during substitution. Specifically:

Define `size t` = number of nodes. Then `size (t[v/x]) ≤ size t * size v`-ish (each occurrence of x replaced by v; but with locally nameless and opening, occurrences are of bvar...). More precisely, if x occurs k times in t, `size(t[v/x]) ≤ size t + k*size v ≤ size t * size v` (assuming sizes ≥ 1).

For beta: `(λx.t) v --> t[v/x]`. The size can grow from `size t + size v + 1` to `size t * size v`. That's an increase. So simple size doesn't work; need a type-weighted measure where the "cost" of a value v at type A is bounded relative to the context.

The classic approach: define `N_T(n)` — for each type T, a function bounding the reduction length of terms of type T in terms of their size n. Then:
- For t : A → B with size n: its reducts...
- For an application `t1 t2 : B` with t1 : A→B: each step either reduces t1 (bounded by N_{A→B}(size t1)) or reduces t2, or performs a beta with a value at type A.

The standard "simple" proof (Howard's "ordinal" / the one in Troelstra's book) uses the fact that the number of steps of a term of type T with size n is bounded by a function `F_T(n)` where:
- F_Bool(n) = n (or n+1)
- F_{A→B}(n) = F_B(F_A(n) * n) or similar. 

Because: a term of type A→B applied to a term of type A: the result has type B. During reduction, the intermediate terms can have size at most ~ (size of original)^(number of steps of the argument) — this is the classic exponential bound. Formally:

Define `B_T : nat -> nat` by:
- B_Bool(n) = n + 1  (number of steps of a Bool term of size n... need to check; a Bool term reduces its subterms)
- B_{A→B}(n) = B_B(n * B_A(n))? 

Hmm, let me think about a rigorous formulation. Actually, there's an elegant classical result: the *height* of the reduction tree of a simply typed term is bounded by a function of the type and the term size using "towers". Let me define:

For a term t, define `sz t := 1 + sum of sizes of subterms` (i.e., number of nodes).

Claim: if `t` is locally closed, has type T, and t -->* t' (multi-step), then `sz t' ≤ G_T(sz t)` for some function G_T.

Then define the measure M(t) = ... hmm, we need a decreasing measure on single steps. If we have a bound on the length and the max size, we could use `M(t) = (B_T(sz t), ...)` lexicographic? **Setting.** `rel T t` for arbitrary terms t:

**Lemma 1 (CR2-ish, "rel closed under step"):** `forall T t t', rel T t -> step t t' -> rel T t'`.
Proof: induction on T.
- Bool: rel Bool t = sn t; sn t gives sn t' via inversion of Acc: `Acc_inv`. ✓
- Arrow A B: rel (A→B) t = sn t /\ Hclos. Need rel (A→B) t' = sn t' /\ (forall v, value v -> rel A v -> rel B (t' v)). sn t' from sn t. For closure: given value v, rel A v. Need rel B (t' v). We have rel B (t v) from Hclos. And `step (t v) (t' v)` via ST_App1 with LC v (from value v → LC v). Then apply IH_B (structural subterm, IH from induction on T). ✓

Note: CR2 for the arrow case uses only IH at B. Induction on ty works since B is a subterm of A→B. In Rocq: `induction T; intros t t' Hrel Hstep; simpl in *`. For T = Ty_Arrow T1 T2, IH is for T1 and T2. Good.

**Lemma 2 (CR1-ish):** `rel T t -> sn t` — by induction on T (base: trivial; arrow: first conjunct).

**Lemma 3 (value LC):** `value v -> locally_closed v`.

Now the **Application Lemma**: `rel (A→B) a -> rel A b -> rel B (a b)`.

Let me look at this from the standard "reducibility candidates" perspective for CBV:

Define `R_T` (a set of terms, closed under steps "CR2" and with the property that if all one-step reducts of t are in R_T and t is SN then t ∈ R_T — this is "CR3", closure under expansion / "backward closure"). Key insight: In CBV, the LR's arrow clause only tests *values* v ∈ R_A. To show t1 t2 ∈ R_B when t2 is not a value, we analyze the reduction of `t1 t2` — any step either reduces t1 (giving t1' t2) or reduces t2 (when t1 is a value). We prove the result by a *nested well-founded induction on sn t1 and sn t2*, using the "expansion" property: if all reducts of a term are in R_B and the term is SN, then the term is in R_B.

So let's prove:

**Lemma (expansion / CR3):** `forall T t, sn t -> (forall t', step t t' -> rel T t') -> rel T t`.

- Bool: sn t and we need sn t. ✓
- Arrow A B: need sn t ✓ and forall v value, rel A v -> rel B (t v). Given v value, rel A v. Need rel B (t v). Apply IH_B with t := t v. Need sn (t v) and forall t'', step (t v) t'' -> rel B t''.
  - sn (t v): hmm, need to show from sn t, value v, rel A v. But this is exactly the "application" lemma again (sn (t v) given sn t and v a value)! Circular?

The measure proof for STLC strong normalization: I recall a clean version. Let's define:

- W_Bool(n) = n (or some polynomial)

The idea: if t : A→B has size n, then during reduction t can be applied to arguments; each argument of type A with size ≤ n_A reduces in ≤ W_A(n_A) steps, and its size stays bounded by W_A(n_A) * n_A or so...

This factorization argument requires induction on the multi-step relation with careful case analysis, plus determinism (or rather, "if t1 t2 -->* x and the first step is on t2, then t1 is a value"). For CBV, the analogous idea: define R_T as a "candidate" closed under **backward reduction** (anti-reduction / expansion). Then:
- Fundamental theorem abs case: R_{A→B}(λx.t) requires (value clause) for all value u ∈ V_A: R_B((λx.t) u), which by backward closure follows from R_B(open t u) — which we get from the IH if we can apply the IH to the body with the extended substitution. ✓

Let's step back and design the cleanest possible LR proof, aiming for a minimal set of lemmas. Let me consider the "V/R" (value/term) style with multi-step, which is the standard CBV logical relation (as in e.g. "Logical Relations for Call-by-value" by Siek et al.):

```
V_Bool(v)  := v = true \/ v = false        (for value v)
V_{A→B}(v) := v = abs T t0 /\ forall u, value u -> V_A(u) -> R_B(open t0 u)
R_T(t)     := sn t /\ forall t', t -->* t' -> value t' -> V_T(t')
```

Hmm, but then the fundamental theorem's app case needs the factorization lemma. That's a big investment.

Actually, here's a classical and quite simple approach: **the "size of the type-directed transliteration"**. For a simply typed term t : T, define a natural number `‖t‖` recursively:
- `‖x‖ = 1`, `‖c‖ = 1`
- `‖λx:A. t‖ = ‖t‖ + 1`? 

and use the type A to bound. The classic result (due to Turing, see e.g. "The Church-Rosser theorem and strong normalization"): define `h(t)` for a typed term via the "level" of the type: for t : T, define `|t|_T` where:
- `|t|_Bool` = number of symbols of t + 1
- `|t|_{A→B}` = ... 

Standard construction: Let `sz(t)` = size. Define for each type T a monotone function `F_T : nat → nat` bounding the length of reduction sequences of terms of type T of size n, with:
- F_Bool(n) = n (since Bool-typed terms reduce their subterms only... but they contain arrow-typed subterms)
Hmm, the mutual recursion is: terms of type Bool have subterms of arrow type (e.g., `if (app f x) then ...`).

The standard measure: define for each term t (with type) a "complexity" `c(t)`:
c(t) = 1 + Σ_{u proper subterm of t} (something) ...

`(t)^` = CBN term: 
- `x^ = x`
- `(λx:A. t)^ = λx:A. t^`

But careful with substitution under binders: `t1^` may have binders; capture-avoiding substitution of t2 for the free var z is fine ✓.
- `true^ = true`, `false^ = false`
- `(if t1 then t2 else t3)^ = if t1^ then t2^ else t3^` (CBN if with call-by-name reduction? Hmm, our CBN target would need if-then-else with lazy semantics: `if t1 then t2 else t3` reduces when t1 --> true, or when t1 --> t1' — that's like CBN.)
- Values: `(abs)^ = abs` etc.

Claim: if `t --> t'` in CBV then `t^ --> t'^` in `-->*` (multi-step) of CBN. Let's check:
- ST_AppAbs: `(λx.t) v --> t[v/x]` in CBV. Translation: `((λx.t) v)^ = (λz. (λx.t^) z) v^ --> (λx.t^) v^ --> t^[v^/x] = (t[v/x])^` (if substitution commutes with translation — needs v to be a value? - ST_If: t1 --> t1' → ✓.
- ST_IfFalse ✓.

So CBV multi-step maps to CBN multi-step: if t -->* t' in CBV then t^ -->* t'^ in CBN. Then if t^ is SN in CBN and t -->* t' ⟹ t^ -->* t'^, we get: SN(t^) implies SN(t)?? Alternative clean approach: `Definition snb t k := exists h, h <= k /\ SNderiv t h` where `SNderiv t h` is a "height-bounded SN derivation": `SNderiv t 0 := False`? Then `SNderiv t k -> sn t` by induction on k: k = 0 impossible? `SNderiv t 0` would be defined as... if we define `SNderiv t 0 := False`, then any term with `SNderiv t 0` is anything (ex falso), and `SNderiv t (S k) -> sn t`: given H : forall t', step t t' -> SNderiv t' k, then sn t via Acc_intro: for all t', step t t' -> Acc t'. From H t' Hstep : SNderiv t' k, by IH (on k) get Acc t' ✓. And SNderiv t 0 = False gives Acc t trivially ✓. 

So: `Fixpoint SNb (t : tm) (k : nat) := match k with 0 => False | S k' => forall t', step t t' -> SNb t' k' end`. Then `SNb t k -> sn t` by induction on k ✓ easy.

Then the measure proof: for each type T, define a function `Bnd T : nat -> nat` such that for any t with size ≤ n and t : T (well-typed in empty context... or with free vars?), `SNb t (Bnd T n)`.

But the problem: proving `SNb t (Bnd T n)` requires analyzing steps, and for the beta case we need a bound on the contractum's size and type. Since the term can be open (with fvars) during the proof? We can restrict to closed terms (LC, no fvars) — but the induction hypothesis for subterms... Hmm.

The measure proof structure: To show `SNb t (Bnd T (size t))` for well-typed t, we'd do induction on the typing derivation. The app case: `t1 t2` with t1 : A→B, t2 : A. We know SNb t1 (Bnd (A→B) n1) and SNb t2 (Bnd A n2). Need SNb (t1 t2) (Bnd B n). Unfolding SNb: for all t', step (t1 t2) t' -> SNb t' (Bnd B n - 1). The reducts: t1' t2 (App1) — need SNb (t1' t2) (Bnd B n - 1): but we know t1' is a reduct of t1, so it satisfies SNb t1' (Bnd (A→B) n1 - 1) — but does it have the same type A→B? Yes (preservation). And its size ≤ n1? Size of t1' could be larger than t1! E.g., beta reduction inside t1 can increase size. Hmm, so the bound function must account for growth: we need size bounds on reducts.

So we need the growth lemma: if t : T, t --> t', size t ≤ n, then size t' ≤ G_T(n) for some function G_T, and the number of steps ≤ B_T(n). Standard.

This is the classic "Howard/Turing" bound. Let's define it:

Define `B_T(n)` = max number of reduction steps for a closed term of type T of size ≤ n. And `G_T(n)` = max size of any reduct of such a term.

Recurrences:
- For T = Bool: A Bool-typed term's steps reduce subterms; subterms can have arrow types with larger sizes. Hmm, so B_Bool depends on B_A for all subterm types A, which are "smaller" types (subterm types in a well-typed term are smaller than the whole type? In STLC, subterm types are subtypes of the enclosing type — yes: for t : T, every subterm of t has a type that is a subterm (in the syntax tree) of T or... hmm, for `t1 t2` with t1 : A→B, the type of the whole is B, and the type A of t2 is not a subterm of B — it's a *part of the type of a subterm*. But A is a subterm of A→B, and A→B is the type of subterm t1. So types of subterms of a well-typed term of type T are subterms of T or of types of subterms... By induction, the set of all types occurring in a well-typed term t : T are subterms of T (since typing is compositional: t1 : A→B where A→B is the type of a subterm of the whole term, and the whole term's type T is B for an application... so A is a subterm of A→B which is the type of a subterm). So yes: all types in a well-typed term are subterms of T, hence have weight ≤ weight(T). So a well-founded recursion on the type structure works if we use functions of the "size of type".

Classic recurrences (I recall from the "simple proof of SN" by van Dalen or the one in "Lambda calculus and combinators" by Hindley-Seldin):
- Define `f_T(n)` = a bound on the length of reduction sequences for terms of type T of size ≤ n:
  - `f_Bool(n) = n + 1`? 
  - `f_{A→B}(n) = f_B(n * (f_A(n) + 2))`? 

Hmm, let me think about whether there's a neat derivation.

The needed properties:
(1) If t : T, size t ≤ n, and t --> t', then size t' ≤ G_T(n) and t' : T, and the remaining steps ≤ B_T(n) - 1 where B_T(n) = 1 + max over steps... 

Let's define mutually:
- `B_T(n)` := bound on the number of steps of a term of type T of size ≤ n.
- `G_T(n)` := bound on the size of any reduct of a term of type T with size ≤ n.

Recurrences:
For a term `t` of type T, a step is either:
- Reduction inside a subterm u of type U: the subterm u --> u'. Then the whole term's size goes from ≤ n to ≤ n + (G_U(n) - size u) ≤ n + G_U(n). And the number of steps: each such step reduces the "sum" over subterms of remaining steps, which is a lexicographic/multiset measure.

The standard way: define the measure `M(t)` = Σ_{u ⊑ t} (something like a weighted remaining bound) and show it decreases.

Hmm, formalizing the multiset ordering is painful. Alternative: define `B_T` such that `M(t) := Σ_{u subterm of t} B_{type(u)}(size t)`? Hmm, this is getting complicated because we'd need to attach types to subterms (via a typing derivation).

**Key difficulty:** The measure proof needs type annotations for subterms of a term. We can carry the typing derivation along, but the "subterm types" of reducts are determined by the reduct's own typing derivation — since typing is syntax-directed (mostly, except T_Abs's context), each well-typed term has an essentially unique type and derivation structure? T_Abs's premise is over all fresh x — the derivation is determined by the term's structure. So typing derivations are determined by the term (up to the choice of L in T_Abs). Hmm, in Rocq, induction on the typing derivation is fine.

This is a serious formalization effort either way.

**Let me reconsider the LR with the contractum clause — I think it's the standard approach and not that bad.** Let's carefully work out the CBV LR proof with the contractum condition, and see all the lemmas needed. I'll try to keep infrastructure minimal by not requiring LC everywhere.

### Detailed LR design

```
Fixpoint rel (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => sn t
  | Ty_Arrow A B =>
      sn t /\
      (forall v, value v -> rel A v -> rel B (tm_app t v)) /\
      (forall t0 v, t = tm_abs A t0 -> value v -> rel A v -> rel B (open t0 v))
  end.
```

Note: the third conjunct could be phrased as `forall v, value v -> rel A v -> rel B (match t with tm_abs _ t0 => open t0 v | _ => t end)`. But the "forall t0, t = tm_abs A t0 -> ..." phrasing is provable.

Hmm, wait. There might be a simpler way to think of it: define a helper "apply" function:
`Definition apply (t v : tm) := match t with tm_abs _ t0 => open t0 v | _ => tm_app t v end.`
Then rel (A→B) t := sn t /\ forall v, value v -> rel A v -> rel B (apply t v) — but that's not enough: for non-abs t, we want `rel B (t v)` (needed to prove SN of `t v` via reducts), and for abs t, we want `rel B (open t0 v)`. Using `apply` gives exactly one of them depending on the shape. Hmm, but for the step analysis of `t v`:
- If t = abs: `t v --> open t0 v` ✓ (apply gives open t0 v ✓), and there are no App1/App2 steps from `abs v`, but `abs v` can also step via App2 if v steps ✓ (handled by induction on v), or via App1? abs is a value, no steps ✓.
- If t is not an abs: `t v` steps via App1 (t --> t') when LC v, or App2 if t is a value (true/false or abs — but not abs since t isn't abs, so t = true/false). Using `apply t v = tm_app t v` ✓ and closure:
  - App1: need rel B (t' v) = rel B (apply t' v) — from IH at t' (need rel (A→B) t' via CR2) ✓ — hmm, but `apply t' v` might be `open t0' v` if t' is an abs! Then we'd need `open t0' v` related, which is fine because apply t' v is defined that way and the IH gives rel B (apply t' v) ✓. But then the step `t' v --> open t0' v`... we don't need to consider it if we're using `apply` semantics carefully. Hmm, but SN of `t v` requires handling the actual term `t v`, whose reducts include `open t0 v` when t is an abs.

This "apply" trick is clever: define `rel (A→B) t := sn t ∧ forall v, value v -> rel A v -> rel B (apply t v)`. Then in the application lemma, the goal `rel B (t1 t2)`: if t1 = abs, `apply t1 t2 = open t0 t2` and the closure clause directly gives it (when t2 is a value) ✓; if t1 is not an abs, `apply t1 t2 = tm_app t1 t2` ✓.

Hmm, but is `rel (A→B)` correct in the sense that we can prove the app lemma? Let's redo the analysis with `apply`:

Goal: `rel B (t1 t2)` given R_{A→B}(t1) and R_A(t2).
Case B = Bool: need sn(t1 t2).
 - Reducts of t1 t2: 
   - t1 --> t1' (with LC t2): need sn(t1' t2). By CR2 at A→B: rel (A→B) t1'. Hmm, now the outer induction on sn t1 gives IH: forall t2, rel A t2 -> rel B (t1' t2) ✓ (as long as we set up the induction statement right).
   - t1 value, t2 --> t2': need sn(t1 t2') — inner induction on sn t2 with t1 fixed & value.
   - t1 = tm_abs A t0, t2 a value: reduct is open t0 t2, need sn(open t0 t2) ✓ from the closure clause: rel (A→B)(abs A t0) gives forall v value, rel A v -> rel B (apply (abs A t0) v) = rel B (open t0 v) ✓ with v := t2 ✓.
Case B = B1→B2: need sn(t1 t2) (same as above; note the closure clause of rel (A→B) at t1 = abs gives rel (B1→B2) (open t0 t2) which gives sn ✓) AND forall v value, rel B1 v -> rel B2 (apply (t1 t2) v).

Reducts of `(t1 t2) v`: App1 steps on the function `t1 t2` (needs LC v ✓), App2 on v when `t1 t2` is a value (never, since it's an app) — so only App1. So `(t1 t2) v --> (x) v` where `t1 t2 --> x`. And we need rel B2 (x v).
 
 So generally we need: **for all terms s with rel B (s) [where B = B1→B2], rel B2 (s v)** — that's just the closure clause of rel B s ✓ directly! But we're trying to prove rel B (t1 t2) which is what gives it. Circular again, but now we can use the well-founded induction: rel B2 ((t1 t2) v) needs rel B (t1 t2)'s second conjunct... which is exactly what we're proving. Hmm, so the circularity persists: to prove `rel B (t1 t2)` (for B an arrow), we need the second conjunct at v, which needs `rel B (t1 t2)` itself.

Hmm, unless we prove `rel B (t1 t2)` by well-founded induction on the term `t1 t2` measured by sn: i.e., prove `rel T s` by Acc-induction on sn s for all s, using the "expand" step: `rel T s` holds if for all s', step s s' -> rel T s' and sn s. Then for s = t1 t2, the reducts are smaller in the SN order, and the closure-at-v conjunct is `rel B2 (s v)` — which is `rel B2` applied to a *different* term `s v` where `s` occurs as a subterm. So we need an induction on s that also covers `s v`. If we strengthen the induction statement to cover "s applied to a stack of arguments", we get the Kripke/spine lemma.

Alternatively, prove rel B2 (s v) via the same expansion idea: rel B2 (s v) holds if sn (s v) and all reducts of `s v` are rel B2. The reducts of `s v` are `s' v` (from s stepping) — and by IH on sn s, since s' is a reduct of s, `rel B (s')` holds, hence its closure gives `rel B2 (s' v)` ✓ — plus (if s is an abs) the beta reduct. But `s = t1 t2` is not an abs, so that case doesn't arise! And `sn (s v)` — likewise from sn s and v a value? Let's see: steps of `s v` (s not an abs): App1 from s steps → need sn (s' v) via IH; App2 if s is a value (not, since app). So sn (s v) follows from sn s by Acc induction (statement: for all s with sn s, for all v value with rel A v, sn (s v)) — ✓ easy by Acc induction since s is never an abs in... wait, s could be an abs in general, but for `s = t1 t2` in this instance it's not.

So the structure suggests: prove a general lemma by Acc-induction on sn s, for s of arrow type (rel (B1→B2) s), that `rel B2 (s v)` for values v — but for s = abs, this needs the contractum clause.

I believe the cleanest formulation is:

**Main Lemma (spine):** For all types A, B, terms s, and "argument spine" — actually let's just do:

and prove it by **Acc-induction on sn s**, with the statement *including the arrow-closure conjunct of the conclusion*, i.e., we prove rel B (s t) for all t, and in the arrow case, the closure sub-goal `rel B2 (apply (s t) v)` — which equals `rel B2 ((s t) v)` since s t is an app. Then we can apply... hmm, `(s t) v` — we want to use the induction hypothesis at s? The IH is at reducts of s. `(s t) v` isn't of the form `s' t`.

Hmm, hmm. But wait: maybe use the expansion/CR3 property: 

`Lemma rel_expand : forall T s, sn s -> (forall s', step s s' -> rel T s') -> rel T s.`

Check by induction on T:
- Bool: sn s ✓.
- Arrow A B: need sn s ✓ and closure: forall v value, rel A v -> rel B (apply s v).

The hypothesis "forall s', step s s' -> rel T s'" gives nothing since abs doesn't step. So `rel_expand` is NOT provable for abs terms at arrow type. 

Right — because at arrow type, the crucial info comes from the body, and the "all reducts" hypothesis is vacuous for values. So `rel_expand` fails.

Let me define the app lemma proof by **Acc induction on sn t1** and separately on sn t2, with the goal `rel B (t1 t2)`. I'll structure the proof as follows:

and the arrow sub-goal needs the claim at `s t` (i.e., `rel (B1→B2) (s t)`) — which is what we're proving, so the proof must be by induction where the instance `s t` is "smaller" than `s`. Along the SN order: s t is NOT a reduct of s. Hmm.

`Claim: forall A B (s : tm), rel (A→B) s -> forall t, rel A t -> rel B (s t)`

by Acc-induction on `sn s`, and in the arrow case, the goal `rel B2 ((s t) v)` can be proven by the *inner structure*: Let's prove it via rel_expand at B2 for the term `(s t) v`, which needs sn and all reducts. In the claim's conclusion I wrote `rel B (s t)` (actual application term). Hmm, so the conclusion should be `rel B (s t)` (actual term), and if s = abs, we show it via... the arrow closure gives rel B (apply s t) = rel B (open t0 t) and then rel B (s t) requires "anti-reduction" (backward closure) which requires determinism... ugh.

Alternatively, formulate the claim in terms of `apply`:

`Claim: forall A B s, rel (A→B) s -> forall t, rel A t -> rel B (apply s t)`.

Hmm, but then in the app case of the fundamental theorem, we need `rel B (tm_app t1 t2)`, not `apply t1 t2`. When t1 is an abs, apply t1 t2 = open t0 t2 ≠ t1 t2. Hmm. ### Full proof plan

**Fundamental theorem:** `forall Gamma t T, Gamma |-- t \in T -> forall sigma, (forall x T, Gamma x = Some T -> rel T (sigma x)) -> rel T (subst sigma t)`.

T_Abs case: `Gamma |-- lambda : T1, t1 \in T1 -> T2`, i.e., t = tm_abs T1 t1. Need rel (T1→T2) (subst sigma (tm_abs T1 t1)) = rel (T1→T2) (tm_abs T1 (subst sigma t1)).
Components:

 (c) closure: forall v, value v -> rel T1 v -> rel T2 (apply (tm_abs T1 (subst sigma t1)) v) = rel T2 (open (subst sigma t1) v). And the contractum conjunct.

Hmm, this "apply vs actual application" mismatch keeps biting. Maybe I should abandon the `apply` trick and use the explicit contractum conjunct, so that rel at arrow type gives BOTH `rel B (t v)` and the contractum. Let's define:

```
rel (A→B) t := lc t /\ sn t /\
  (forall v, value v -> rel A v -> rel B (tm_app t v)) /\
  (forall t0 v, t = tm_abs A t0 -> value v -> rel A v -> rel B (open t0 v))
```

Then all the closure info is available in both forms, and we don't need to bridge. The cost: CR2 must maintain the contractum clause (as analyzed, doable with the step_abs_lc lemma), and the fundamental theorem's abs case must establish both clauses (both are the same statement essentially: for t = abs T1 (subst sigma t1), the app clause is rel B (app t v) and the contractum clause is rel B (open t0 v) with t0 = subst sigma t1; from rel B (open (subst sigma t1) v) we get rel B (app t v) by expansion/CR2 — hmm, `app (abs T1 t0) v --> open t0 v` so by... backwards: rel B (open t0 v) + step → rel B (app ... v)? So let's go with:

```
Fixpoint rel (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  locally_closed t /\
  sn t /\
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => True
  | Ty_Arrow A B =>
      (forall v, value v -> rel A v -> rel B (tm_app t v)) /\
      (forall t0, t = tm_abs A t0 ->
         forall v, value v -> rel A v -> rel B (open t0 v))
  end.
```

Note: at Ty_Bool, `rel Bool t = lc t ∧ sn t ∧ True` ✓.

**Alternative simplification: prove the fundamental theorem by induction on the typing derivation, with a strengthened induction hypothesis that includes the "all reducts" property.** This is the classic "Tait's method with a candidate of reducible terms closed under expansion". Let's think about the structure:

For each type T, define `R T` as a set of terms satisfying:
- R T ⊆ LC ∩ SN
- closure under step (CR2)
- if a non-abs term t has all reducts in R T (and t is LC, SN) then t ∈ R T (CR3/expansion).

And then prove: for well-typed t and substitution sigma, `subst sigma t ∈ R T` by induction on typing. The abs case: to show `tm_abs T1 t0[σ] ∈ R (T1→T2)`, we need its body property: for all value v ∈ R T1, `open t0[σ] v ∈ R T2` — which follows from the IH on the body (with the extended substitution) — but ALSO the app-property: `(tm_abs T1 t0[σ]) v ∈ R T2`. Since `(abs) v --> open t0[σ] v` and `open t0[σ] v ∈ R T2` and the abs-app is a non-abs term whose only reduct is the contractum, expansion gives it ✓. So R (T1→T2) should be defined as: terms t such that (i) LC, SN, (ii) for all value v ∈ R T1: `t v ∈ R T2` — hmm, but for t = abs, `t v` steps to the contractum, so expansion at R T2 for `t v` requires the contractum ∈ R T2 ✓ which is what the IH gives. So define:

`R (A→B) t := lc t ∧ sn t ∧ forall v, value v -> R A v -> R B (tm_app t v)`.

Then for t = abs: to show R (A→B) t, need for all value v ∈ R A: R B (t v). Use expansion at B on `t v`: reducts are just `open t0 v` (since t and v are values) → need R B (open t0 v) — from the body's property ✓. And sn (t v) ✓ (reduct is rel → sn). And lc ✓. So we need the body's property `R B (open t0 v)` to be established by the IH on the typing of the body, which gives it for the extended substitution ✓.

This is the same as before but without the explicit contractum conjunct (using expansion to derive the app property). The key: expansion at B for `t v` where `t v` is a non-abs term with exactly one reduct. So the expansion lemma is doing the work.

```
  | B, [] => rel B s
```

Alternatively define the app lemma in terms of `app_spine s args` (fold-left application):

```
Fixpoint apps (s : tm) (args : list tm) : tm :=
  match args with [] => s | t :: rest => apps (tm_app s t) rest end.
```

and the lemma: `rel T s -> (list of args matching T) -> rel T' (apps s args)`. The "matching" predicate needs types: 

```
Inductive typed_spine : tm -> ty -> list tm -> ty -> Prop :=
  | ts_nil : forall T s, typed_spine s T [] T
  | ts_cons : forall s A B T rest t, typed_spine s B rest T -> typed_spine s (A→B) (t::rest) T
```
with the extra condition `rel A t` for each. Hmm, we can bundle: define

```
Inductive spine_rel : tm -> ty -> list tm -> ty -> Prop :=
  | sr_nil : forall s T, rel T s -> spine_rel s T [] T
  | sr_cons : forall s A B rest t T, rel A t -> spine_rel (tm_app s t) B rest T -> spine_rel s (A→B) (t::rest) T
```

and prove: `forall s T args T', spine_rel s T args T' -> rel T' (apps s args)` — by induction on spine_rel. Hmm, the sr_cons case: IH gives `rel T' (apps (s t) rest)` and we need `rel T' (apps s (t::rest))` — which is the same term by definition ✓ (apps s (t::rest) = apps (s t) rest = apps (tm_app s t) rest ✓ definitionally). But the sr_cons's premise `spine_rel (tm_app s t) B rest T` requires `rel B (tm_app s t)` when rest is empty — and that's the app lemma itself! Circular. Hmm.

OK so let's step back and think about the cleanest way to organize this. 

**Observation**: The real difficulty is essentially the standard "the LR at arrow type must be a reducibility candidate closed under expansion, and the app lemma needs the argument to be evaluated". Let me look at this from a different angle: define the LR using the **evaluation semantics** with multi-step to values. This is the standard approach for CBV logical relations and avoids the spine issue because the arrow clause is stated "for all values v related, the application v s is related" and the reduction is factored.

Let's define:

```
Definition halts (t v : tm) := value v /\ multi step t v.

Fixpoint V (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=   (* for values t *)
  match T with
  | Bool => t = tm_true \/ t = tm_false
  | A→B => exists t0, t = tm_abs A t0 /\ forall v, value v -> V A v -> R B (open t0 v)
  end
with R (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  sn t /\ forall v, multi step t v -> V T v.
```
Hmm, mutual fixpoint with an inner forall — need to define V first (structural on T), with R occurring on subterms: `V (A→B) t` refers to `R B` ✓ subterm, and `R T t` refers to `V T` ✓ same type. Can't do mutual recursion where R T refers to V T (same argument) but V (A→B) refers to R B (subterm) — should be fine as a nested fixpoint on T: define `RV T : (tm -> Prop) * (tm -> Prop)` as a fixpoint on T. In Rocq:

```
Fixpoint RV (T : ty) : tm -> Prop * tm -> Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => (fun t => t = tm_true \/ t = tm_false, fun t => sn t /\ forall v, multi step t v -> value v /\ (v = tm_true \/ v = tm_false))
  ...
```
Hmm, getting messy with pairs. Let's avoid mutual recursion: define R first using V, but V depends on R... Alternatively, define R without V by using R itself at the value:

`R T t := sn t /\ forall v, multi step t v -> value v -> V T v` — where V is defined via R at subterms. So we can define `rel T t` by Fixpoint on T where:
- rel Bool t := sn t ∧ forall v, multi step t v -> value v -> (v = true ∨ v = false) — hmm, do we need the "value" condition? A term of type Bool reduces only to true/false, but for arbitrary terms... Let's think about whether we need this clause at Bool. Probably for the if-case: we need to know something about the scrutinee. Suppose t : Bool, and `if t then a else b` — the reduction: if t is a value (true/false) → branch; else t steps. So for SN of `if t a b`, we need: sn t, sn a, sn b — actually we don't need to know that t reduces to true/false; if t is stuck, the if is stuck, fine. If t reduces, we handle by induction. So maybe the Bool clause needs only sn? But then, the fundamental theorem's if-case must prove `R T (if t1 t2 t3)` which requires (for arrow T) the app-clause: forall v, value v, R T1 v -> R T2 ((if t1 t2 t3) v) — with the reducts of `(if ...) v`: App1 on the if → `(if t1' t2 t3) v`, or App2 if the if is a value (impossible), or beta if the if is an abs (impossible). So it's handled by the same reasoning. Fine, so maybe the if case is manageable.

The "reduces to a value" clause at Bool: is it needed for the app lemma? - The factorization lemma: if `(s t) -->* v` with v a value, then ∃vs, vs', s -->* vs ∧ value vs ∧ t -->* vs' ∧ value vs' ∧ (open t0 vs' or vs vs') -->* v. Hmm, and also the App1 case: s -->* vs requires s to not get stuck.

Hmm, let me reconsider the single-relation approach with expansion, handling the spine issue. Actually, maybe the spine issue can be solved elegantly:

**Key realization:** In the app lemma, to prove `rel B (s t)` we use expansion at B on `s t`. The needed sub-goal `rel B2 ((s t) v)` (for B = B1→B2) can be proven by expansion at B2 on `(s t) v`. Its reducts are `x v` where `s t --> x`. For each such x, we need `rel B2 (x v)`. Now, x is a reduct of `s t`, so by expansion (already used at B), x satisfies `rel B x` — but that's "the goal" we're proving by expansion. Hmm, but here's the thing: the expansion lemma is an *induction on SN of the term*, so if we state expansion as:

`rel_expand : forall T s, lc s -> sn s -> non_abs s -> (forall s', step s s' -> rel T s') -> rel T s`

then within its proof (at T = A→B), the sub-goal rel B2 ((s) v) requires expansion at B2 on the term `s v`, whose reducts are `s' v`. For each `s' v`, we need `rel B2 (s' v)`; by rel_expand at B2 (recursively) applied to `s' v`, we need its reducts related, etc. The recursion is on sn — but with respect to which term? We need a well-founded measure that decreases: `s v` is a bigger term than `s`, and `s' v` involves s' which is smaller than s in SN order. So the recursion isn't simply on sn s. However, we can *prove a combined statement by Acc induction on sn s*:

`Claim(s) := forall (non_abs s), lc s, (forall s', step s s' -> rel T s') -> P(s)` where P(s) = rel T s... 

I think the cleanest is to prove the expansion lemma by **structural induction on T with a nested Acc induction on s**, where the statement at arrow type is:

`E_arrow(s) := non_abs s -> lc s -> (forall s', step s s' -> rel (A→B) s') -> rel (A→B) s`

proven by Acc induction on sn s. Then inside, the app-clause needs `rel B (s v)` for values v: use IH_B (expansion at B, structural subterm) applied to the term `s v` — which requires (i) non_abs ✓, (ii) lc ✓, (iii) sn (s v) — need to prove, (iv) all reducts of `s v` are rel B: reducts are `s' v` with s --> s' → need rel B (s' v) — use expansion at B (IH_B) again on `s' v`: it requires sn (s' v) and all reducts... Hmm, this recursion on `s' v` is on a term involving s' which is a reduct of s, so the Acc IH at s' might apply if we generalize the statement to include arbitrary values applied. So the statement for the Acc induction should be:

`E(s) := forall v, value v -> rel A v -> rel B (s v)` — hmm, but that's exactly the app-clause. Hmm! But the hypothesis `forall s', step s s' -> rel (A→B) s'` gives at s' the app-clause `rel B (s' v)` ✓ directly (since rel (A→B) s' includes its app clause). So:

To prove `rel B (s v)` by expansion at B, we need all reducts of `s v` rel B: reducts are `s' v` (App1) → by hypothesis rel (A→B) s' → its app-clause at v gives rel B (s' v) ✓ — no recursion needed!! Because the app-clause of rel (A→B) s' gives it directly. And sn (s v): reducts are sn (s' v) — from rel (A→B) s' → sn (s' v) ✓. And App2/AppAbs don't apply (s non_abs; v value doesn't step). So sn (s v) follows from Acc_intro with all reducts sn ✓ — we can prove `sn (s v)` via `Acc_intro` directly since each reduct `s' v` has sn from rel (A→B) s' ✓.

So the expansion lemma's arrow case needs NO nested Acc induction! Let's re-examine:

```
Lemma rel_expand : forall T s, non_abs s -> locally_closed s -> sn s ->
   (forall s', step s s' -> rel T s') -> rel T s.
Proof.
  induction T; intros s Hna Hlc Hsn Hred.
  - (* Bool *) simpl. auto.
  - (* Arrow A B *) simpl. split; [exact Hlc | split; [exact Hsn | ...]].
    + (* app clause: forall v, value v -> rel A v -> rel B (tm_app s v) *)
      intros v Hv Hrelv.
      apply (IHT2 (tm_app s v)).   (* IHT2 : expansion at B *)
      * (* non_abs *) simpl. auto.   (* tm_app is not an abs *)
      * (* lc *) constructor; [exact Hlc | apply rel_lc; exact Hrelv].  (* need rel → lc *)
      * (* sn *) apply Acc_intro. intros x Hx. inversion Hx; subst.
          -- ST_AppAbs: s = tm_abs ... contradicts Hna.
          -- ST_App1: x = tm_app s' v. Apply rel_sn with (T:=B). apply Hred? Hmm: Hred s' H : rel (A→B) s'; its app-clause at v gives rel B (s' v) → sn ✓.
          -- ST_App2: requires value s (s non_abs, so s = true/false) and step v v' — but v is a value, contradiction ✓ (need lemma value_no_step).
      * (* all reducts of s v are rel B *) intros x Hx. inversion Hx; subst.
          -- ST_AppAbs: contradicts Hna (s = abs).
          -- ST_App1: x = s' v; from Hred s' H : rel (A→B) s'; apply its app clause ✓.
          -- ST_App2: v steps — contradicts value.
    + (* beta clause: forall t0, s = tm_abs A t0 -> ... *) intros t0 Heq. exfalso. apply Hna. exact Heq. (need non_abs def)
Qed.
```

Wait, but there's a subtlety in the "all reducts" sub-goal: `rel_expand` at B requires `forall x, step (s v) x -> rel B x` ✓ handled. And sn (s v) ✓. Looks like it works! Great, so the expansion lemma is provable by structural induction on T (no Acc nesting), given:
- `rel_sn : rel T t -> sn t`, `rel_lc : rel T t -> locally_closed t`.
- `value_no_step : value v -> ~ step v v'`.
- `non_abs` facts.

**Lemma sn_app:** `forall A B s, rel (A→B) s -> forall t, rel A t -> sn (tm_app s t)`.
Proof: destruct rel (A→B) s → sns; revert; Acc-induct on sns. Statement: `rel (A→B) s -> forall t, rel A t -> sn (s t)`.
Given HrelS, t, HrelT. Apply Acc_intro; intros x Hx; inversion Hx:
- ST_AppAbs: s = abs A t0, t value, x = open t0 t0? (x = open t0 t where the abs is `tm_abs T t0`, T = A). Need sn (open t0 t): from HrelS's beta-clause applied to (t0) refl Hv HrelT → rel B (open t0 t) → sn ✓.
- ST_App1: x = s' t, with s --> s'. Need sn (s' t): apply the outer IH at s' (step s s' ✓) with rel (A→B) s' (CR2 ✓) and HrelT ✓.
- ST_App2: x = s t', s value, t --> t'. Need sn (s t'): inner induction needed. So we need the inner Acc induction on sn t.

So structure: outer Acc on sn s, and inside, we need `forall t, rel A t -> sn (s t)`. Prove this inner statement by Acc on sn t:
`IHinner : forall t', step t t' -> rel A t' -> sn (s t')` — then ST_App2 case: apply IHinner with rel A t' from CR2 ✓; and ST_App1 case uses the outer IH (which is available and independent of t) ✓; ST_AppAbs uses HrelS ✓.
So:

```
Lemma sn_app : forall A B s, rel (A→B) s -> forall t, rel A t -> sn (tm_app s t).
Proof.
  intros A B s HrelS.
  destruct HrelS as (lcs & sns & Hcapp & Hcbeta).
  revert lcs Hcapp Hcbeta.
  induction sns as [s HstepS IHS].
  intros lcs Hcapp Hcbeta t HrelT.
  destruct HrelT as (lct & snt & restA).
  revert lct restA.
  induction snt as [t HstepT IHT].
  intros lct restA.
  apply Acc_intro. intros x Hx. inversion Hx as [T0 t0 v HlcAbs Hv | t1 t1' t2 Hst Hlc2 | v1 t2 t2' Hv1 Hst2]; subst.
  ...
```

Hmm hmm. OK let me think about the spine issue once more. Maybe a cleaner way: **prove the app lemma by induction on the SN of `s t` — no...**

Let's think about the structure of the proof from a semantic standpoint: to show `rel B (s t)`, we need to show that every reduct of `s t` is rel B and sn. The reducts are `s' t`, `s t'`, `open t0 t`. Each is "smaller" in a well-founded sense:
- `s' t`: s' is a reduct of s.
- `s t'`: t' is a reduct of t (when s is a value).
- `open t0 t`: contractum, handled by the beta clause ✓ (no recursion).

So a nested induction on (sn s, sn t) suffices to prove `rel B (s t)` for the "base" B = Bool? No wait, for B = Bool, rel B (s t) = lc ∧ sn ∧ True. The sn part needs exactly the above analysis ✓. For B = Arrow, rel B (s t) needs additionally the app-clause `rel B2 ((s t) v)` for values v with rel B1 v — and here's the crux: `(s t) v`'s reducts are `(s' t) v`, `(s t') v`, `(open t0 t) v`. The first two are handled by the nested IH *if the statement is strengthened to cover spines*: e.g., IH at s' gives rel B (s' t) whose app-clause gives rel B2 ((s' t) v) ✓!! And the third, `(open t0 t) v`: we have rel B (open t0 t) from the beta clause, whose app-clause gives rel B2 ((open t0 t) v) ✓!!

So: to prove the app-clause for rel B (s t) at v, use the expansion lemma at B2 on the term `(s t) v`, needing:
- lc ✓, non_abs ✓
- sn ((s t) v): reducts are (i) `(s' t) v` → sn? From rel B (s' t) (outer IH) → hmm, rel B (s' t) is an arrow-type rel; does it give sn ((s' t) v)? Its app-clause gives rel B2 ((s' t) v) → sn ✓. (ii) `(s t') v` → from inner IH: rel B (s t') → app-clause → rel B2 ((s t') v) → sn ✓. (iii) `(open t0 t) v` → from Hcbeta: rel B (open t0 t) → app-clause → rel B2 (...) → sn ✓.
- all reducts of `(s t) v` are rel B2: (i),(ii),(iii) as above ✓.

So everything works with the nested induction, PROVIDED we have the outer/inner IHs giving `rel B (s' t)` and `rel B (s t')`. And these come from the same lemma statement `P(s,t) := rel (A→B) s -> rel A t -> rel B (s t)` — with the outer Acc induction on sn s and inner Acc on sn t. And the arrow-closure sub-goal is handled by expansion at B2 relying on P at (s', t) and (s, t') — which the IHs provide ✓. And the "sn ((s t) v)" sub-goal also relies on those ✓.

    - sn ((s t) v): reducts:

So the reduct is `x v` where `s t --> x`. I need `sn (x v)`. What do I know about x? `s t --> x` means x is a reduct of `s t`. Since we've *already established* `rel B (s t)` before this sub-goal? NO — we're in the middle of proving it (via expansion, which needs all reducts rel). But: the expansion lemma's premise "forall x, step (s t) x -> rel B x" is available as a hypothesis in this sub-proof! So `rel B x` ✓ for any reduct x of `s t`. Then rel B x's app-clause gives rel B2 (x v) → sn ✓.
      - ST_App2 on `s t` (requires `s t` a value — impossible since it's an app) ✓.
      - ST_AppAbs: requires `s t = tm_abs ...` — impossible ✓.
      So the only reducts are `x v` with `s t --> x`, handled by the expansion hypothesis ✓.
    - all reducts rel B2: same ✓.
  - If B = Bool: no app-clause.

So the key: when proving `rel B (s t)` via `rel_expand at B`, the hypothesis `Hreds : forall x, step (s t) x -> rel B x` is exactly what's needed for the arrow sub-goal's reducts. 

So the structure works out. Similarly, `sn ((s t) v)` for the expansion at B2 uses Hreds ✓.

Wait, but I need to double check the "sn (s t)" requirement for expansion at B: it's proven via Acc_intro from the reduct cases using IHS/IHT/Hcbeta ✓ — no circularity since these are all from the induction hypotheses or the beta clause.

This looks workable. Let me also handle the fundamental theorem.

### Fundamental theorem

```
Theorem fundamental : forall Gamma t T, <{ Gamma |-- t \in T }> ->
  forall sigma, (forall x T, Gamma x = Some T -> rel T (sigma x)) -> rel T (subst sigma t).
```

Cases:
- T_Var: `Gamma x = Some T`, subst sigma (tm_fvar x) = sigma x → apply Hsigma ✓.
- T_True/T_False: subst = tm_true; rel Bool tm_true = lc ∧ sn ∧ True ✓ (true is a value → sn, lc ✓).
- T_Abs: as analyzed. Need x fresh not in L ∪ fv t1. Use the premise to get `x |-> T1 ; Gamma |-- open t1 (fvar x) \in T2`, IH with sigma' = cons x v sigma, then rewrite `subst sigma' (open t1 (fvar x)) = open (subst sigma t1) v`.
  Sub-goals for rel (T1→T2) (tm_abs T1 (subst sigma t1)):
  - lc: has_type_lc on the abs + lc_subst ✓.
  - sn: abs is a value → sn ✓.
  - app-clause: forall v value, rel T1 v -> rel T2 (tm_app (tm_abs T1 (subst sigma t1)) v). Prove via expansion at T2 on that app term:
    - non_abs ✓, lc ✓.
    - sn: its reducts: hmm, `(abs T1 t0) v` where t0 = subst sigma t1: reducts are (a) `open t0 v` (AppAbs, v value), (b) none via App1/App2 (abs is a value, v is a value). So sn via Acc_intro: reduct `open t0 v` must be sn → from rel T2 (open t0 v) which is the beta-clause...
    - all reducts rel T2: the only reduct is `open t0 v` ✓ which we have.
  - beta-clause: forall t0, tm_abs T1 (subst sigma t1) = tm_abs T1 t0 -> forall v value, rel T1 v -> rel T2 (open t0 v). Inversion gives t0 = subst sigma t1; goal: rel T2 (open (subst sigma t1) v) ✓ from the IH as described.
  
  So both clauses reduce to `rel T2 (open (subst sigma t1) v)` ✓.

- T_App: subst sigma (tm_app t1 t2) = tm_app (subst sigma t1) (subst sigma t2). We have IH1 : rel (T1→T2) (subst sigma t1) and IH2 : rel T1 (subst sigma t2). Apply rel_app ✓.

- T_If: subst = tm_if (subst sigma t1) (subst sigma t2) (subst sigma t3). Need rel T (if a b c) where a : Bool, b, c : T. Hmm! This is a new lemma: `rel Bool a -> rel T b -> rel T c -> rel T (tm_if a b c)`.

  Let's think about this. rel Bool a = lc a ∧ sn a ∧ (forall v, multi step a v -> value v -> (v = true ∨ v = false)). Hmm, do we actually need the multi-step clause? Let's think about proving `rel T (if a b c)`.
  - lc ✓, sn (if a b c): reducts:
    - ST_IfTrue: a = true → b: rel T b → sn ✓.
    - ST_IfFalse: a = false → c: rel T c → sn ✓.
    - ST_If: a --> a' (LC b, LC c) → if a' b c: need sn — by Acc induction on sn a with statement `forall a', step a a' -> sn (if a' b c)`, we get IHS a' Hstep : ... hmm, the statement for the Acc induction would be: `forall a, sn a -> (some condition) -> forall b c, rel T b -> rel T c -> rel T (if a b c)`? Let's define:
    
    `Lemma rel_if : forall T a, rel Bool a -> forall b c, rel T b -> rel T c -> rel T (tm_if a b c)`.
    Proof by Acc induction on sn a (from rel Bool a): statement `rel Bool a -> forall b c, rel T b -> rel T c -> rel T (tm_if a b c)`.
    Given HrelA, b, c, HrelB, HrelC. Use expansion at T on `tm_if a b c`:
    - non_abs ✓, lc ✓.
    - sn: Acc_intro; reducts:
      - ST_IfTrue (a = true): `sn b` from HrelB ✓.
      - ST_IfFalse: `sn c` ✓.
      - ST_If (a --> a'): apply IHS at a' with rel Bool a' (CR2) → gives rel T (if a' b c) → sn ✓.
      - Other rules? ST_App1/2/AppAbs don't match `tm_if` ✓.
    - all reducts rel T: same three cases ✓ (ST_IfTrue → HrelB, ST_IfFalse → HrelC, ST_If → IHS).
    Then rel_expand at T gives rel T (if a b c) including the app-clause. If T = B1→B2, the app-clause: forall v value, rel B1 v -> rel B2 ((if a b c) v). Prove via expansion at B2 on `(if a b c) v` (non_abs ✓):
      - sn: reducts of `(if a b c) v`: ST_App1 on the if → `(if a' b c) v` where `if a b c --> if a' b c`; we have the expansion hypothesis "all reducts of the if are rel T" → rel T (if a' b c) → its app-clause gives rel B2 ✓ → sn ✓. ST_App2: the if is not a value ✓. AppAbs: the if is not an abs ✓.
      - all reducts rel B2: same ✓.
    So the `rel_if` lemma works with expansion, and note: do we even need the multi-step value clause of rel Bool a? Looking at the proof: we only used lc, sn, CR2! We never needed "a reduces to true or false". Interesting — because the `if` is stuck when a is stuck, which is fine for SN. And for the app-clause, `(if ...) v` is stuck when the if is stuck ✓. So rel Bool a can simply be lc ∧ sn. 

Hmm wait, but careful: the rel at Bool = lc ∧ sn ∧ True. In `rel_if`, when a = tm_true, `rel Bool a` gives nothing special, but ST_IfTrue directly applies since a is syntactically true ✓. So indeed no need for the "reduces to true/false" clause. 

So the whole LR is:

```
Fixpoint rel (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  locally_closed t /\
  sn t /\
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => True
  | Ty_Arrow A B =>
      (forall v, value v -> rel A v -> rel B (tm_app t v)) /\
      (forall t0, t = tm_abs A t0 -> forall v, value v -> rel A v -> rel B (open t0 v))
  end.
```

Hmm, wait. Now do we actually need the beta-clause as a separate conjunct? With `rel_expand`, the app-clause for an abs term t: `forall v, value v -> rel A v -> rel B (tm_app t v)` — we proved it via expansion from the contractum. So the beta-clause is derivable from the app-clause using expansion (since `t v --> open t0 v` when t = abs and v value, and rel B (t v) + CR2 gives rel B (open t0 v) ✓ — assuming LC of the abs which we have from lc t ✓).

So maybe define rel at arrow type as just:
```
rel (A→B) t := lc t /\ sn t /\ forall v, value v -> rel A v -> rel B (tm_app t v)
```
and derive the beta-clause as a lemma (rel_beta) using CR2 and the step `t v --> open t0 v`:
- Given rel (A→B) t, t = tm_abs A t0, value v, rel A v. Then rel B (t v) from the app-clause. And `step (t v) (open t0 v)` via ST_AppAbs with `locally_closed (tm_abs A t0)` (from lc t and t = abs) and value v ✓. So CR2 at B gives rel B (open t0 v) ✓.

Then in the fundamental theorem's abs case, we need the app-clause `forall v, rel B (tm_app (tm_abs T1 t0) v)` — proven by expansion from the contractum `open t0 v` ✓ as described.

That's cleaner. So rel is:

```
Fixpoint rel (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  locally_closed t /\
  sn t /\
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => True
  | Ty_Arrow A B => forall v, value v -> rel A v -> rel B (tm_app t v)
  end.
```

Three components: `rel_lc`, `rel_sn`, `rel_app_clause`.

Now let's list all the lemmas needed:

**A. SN basics.**
1. `sn t := Acc step t`.

5. `value_no_step : value v -> ~ step v t'`.
6. `sn_acc_intro : (forall t', step t t' -> sn t') -> sn t`.

**B. LC basics.**

9. `lc_open : lc_at (S k) t -> lc_at k u -> lc_at k (open_rec k u t)`? General lemma: `forall k t u, lc_at (S k) t -> lc_at k u -> lc_at k (open_rec k u t)`. Prove by induction on t (generalizing k). Should hold: for bvar i: if i = k then u (lc_at k u ✓) else bvar i with i < S k, i ≠ k → i < k ✓. 10. `lc_subst : locally_closed t -> (forall x, locally_closed (sigma x)) -> locally_closed (subst sigma t)`. Actually more generally `lc_at k t -> (forall x, lc_at k (sigma x)) -> lc_at k (subst sigma t)` ✓ easy induction.

The result of open_rec k u t has bvars from t (those ≠ k) plus bvars of u. So if u has bvars... hmm, if u = fvar x, no bvars. Then lc_at k (open_rec k u t) means all bvars of t other than k are < k, and bvar k itself must be... hmm, bvar k occurrences are replaced by u. **C. Substitution lemmas.**

14. `subst_id : subst (fun x => tm_fvar x) t = t`? Maybe not needed.
15. `open_subst` combination: `subst (cons x v sigma) (open t (tm_fvar x)) = open (subst sigma t) v` given `~ In x (fv t)`:
    subst sigma' (open_rec 0 (fvar x) t) = open_rec 0 (subst sigma' (fvar x)) (subst sigma' t) = open_rec 0 v (subst sigma' t) = open_rec 0 v (subst sigma t) (by 13) ✓.

   Proof by induction on T. Bool: trivial. Arrow A B:

   Wait, I realize the outer statement should include the inner Acc as part of the induction: `forall s, sn s -> rel (A→B) s -> forall t, rel A t -> rel B (s t)`. Induction on sn s.    Let me write the proof sketch concretely:
   ```
   Lemma rel_app : forall A B (s : tm), rel (A→B) s -> forall t, rel A t -> rel B (tm_app s t).
   Proof.
     intros A B s HrelS.
     destruct HrelS as (lcs & sns & Happ).
     revert lcs Happ.
     induction sns as [s HstepS IHS].
     intros lcs Happ t HrelT.

     intros lct HrestT.
     (* Now: IHS : forall s', step s s' -> lc s' -> (forall v, value v -> rel A v -> rel B (s' v)) -> forall t, rel A t -> rel B (s' t) *)

     - (* lc *) constructor; auto.
     - (* sn *) apply Acc_intro; intros x Hx; inversion Hx; subst.
       + (* AppAbs: s = tm_abs T0 t0, value t, x = open t0 t *) apply rel_sn with (T:=B). apply Happ; auto.

           apply IHS with (t := t); auto.   (* need rel (A→B) s' = (lc s', sn s', Happ s') — hmm, I reverted those! *)

     - (* all reducts *) intros x Hx; inversion Hx; subst; ...
   ```
   
   Hmm, problem: after `revert lcs Happ` and induction, `IHS` gives `rel B (s' t)` only if I supply `rel (A→B) s'`'s components — but I reverted them as separate hypotheses, so IHS takes lc s', and Happ for s'. Also, for the fundamental theorem, we need `fv` (free atoms of a term) as a list. Define:

```
Fixpoint fv (t : tm) : list atom :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar _ => []
  | tm_fvar x => [x]
  | tm_app t1 t2 => fv t1 ++ fv t2
  | tm_abs _ t1 => fv t1
  | tm_true | tm_false => []
  | tm_if t1 t2 t3 => fv t1 ++ fv t2 ++ fv t3
  end.
```

Now, `subst`:
```
Fixpoint subst (sigma : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => sigma x
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (subst sigma t1) (subst sigma t2)
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs T (subst sigma t1)
  | tm_true => tm_true
  | tm_false => tm_false
  | tm_if t1 t2 t3 => tm_if (subst sigma t1) (subst sigma t2) (subst sigma t3)
  end.
```

If B is an arrow, `rel B (s t)` includes the app-clause. How does rel_expand prove the app-clause? It does so *internally* using IHB on `s v` (where s is the term in question = `tm_app s t`, and v is the argument). Right: rel_expand's proof at arrow type B = B1→B2 takes s (the term) and needs rel B2 (tm_app s v) — proven by rel_expand at B2 applied to `s v`, requiring sn (s v) and all reducts of `s v` rel B2. The "all reducts" for `s v` (where s = `tm_app s0 t`) is via Hred (the expansion hypothesis for the term `tm_app s0 t`) ✓ — Hred : forall x, step (s0 t) x -> rel B x, and the reducts of `(s0 t) v` are `x v` for `s0 t --> x`, giving rel B x → app-clause → rel B2 (x v) ✓.

So rel_expand at B, when B is an arrow, internally calls rel_expand at B2 with the term `s v` — needs `sn (s v)`: proven inside rel_expand by Acc_intro using Hred ✓ (App1 case: rel B x → app-clause → rel B2 (x v) → sn ✓; App2: v value doesn't step ✓; AppAbs: s non_abs ✓). ✓ All good.

So in `rel_app`, the `rel_expand B (tm_app s t)` call handles everything, including the arrow-closure, using its internal recursion at type B2. 

Now let's also double check `rel_expand`'s IH usage: it's by structural induction on T. For T = Arrow A B, IHB : expansion at B. And the app-clause needs `rel A v` (given) and calls IHB ✓. Fine.

Alright. What about `rel_if`? Similar: `rel_expand T (tm_if a b c)` with sn and all-reducts. ✓.

Now, one more check on `rel_cr2` for A→B: it uses the app-clause of rel (A→B) t: `rel B (tm_app t v)`, then step to `tm_app t' v` via ST_App1 (needs LC v ✓ from value_lc), then IH at B ✓. Fine.

Now let's handle the substitution lemmas:

```
Fixpoint fv (t : tm) : list atom :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar _ => nil
  | tm_fvar x => cons x nil
  | tm_app t1 t2 => app (fv t1) (fv t2)
  | tm_abs _ t1 => fv t1
  | tm_true => nil
  | tm_false => nil
  | tm_if t1 t2 t3 => app (fv t1) (app (fv t2) (fv t3))
  end.

Fixpoint subst (sigma : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => sigma x
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (subst sigma t1) (subst sigma t2)
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs T (subst sigma t1)
  | tm_true => tm_true
  | tm_false => tm_false
  | tm_if t1 t2 t3 => tm_if (subst sigma t1) (subst sigma t2) (subst sigma t3)
  end.

Definition cons (x : atom) (v : tm) (sigma : atom -> tm) : atom -> tm :=
  fun y => if Nat.eqb x y then v else sigma y.
```

`subst_open` bridge lemma:
```
Lemma subst_open_fvar : forall t x v sigma,
  ~ In x (fv t) ->
  subst (cons x v sigma) (open t (tm_fvar x)) = open (subst sigma t) v.
Proof.
  intros t x v sigma Hfresh.
  unfold open. rewrite subst_open_rec.
  simpl. (* subst (cons x v sigma) (tm_fvar x) = cons x v sigma x = if eqb x x then v else ... = v *)
  rewrite subst_cons_irrel; auto.
  (* need: open_rec 0 v (subst (cons x v sigma) t) = open_rec 0 v (subst sigma t) — from subst_cons_irrel *)
Qed.
```
Order: `subst sigma' (open_rec 0 (tm_fvar x) t) = open_rec 0 (subst sigma' (tm_fvar x)) (subst sigma' t)`. `subst sigma' (tm_fvar x)` = sigma' x = if eqb x x then v else sigma x = v ✓ (`Nat.eqb_refl`). Then `subst sigma' t = subst sigma t` by subst_cons_irrel ✓. So the RHS becomes `open_rec 0 v (subst sigma t)` = `open (subst sigma t) v` ✓.

Now the big rel part. Let me write it out.

```
Definition non_abs (t : tm) : Prop :=
  match t with
  | tm_abs _ _ => False
  | _ => True
  end.

Lemma non_abs_neq : forall s A t0, non_abs s -> s <> tm_abs A t0.
```

```
Fixpoint rel (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  locally_closed t /\
  sn t /\
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => True
  | Ty_Arrow A B => forall v, value v -> rel A v -> rel B (tm_app t v)
  end.
```

Careful with the Fixpoint guard condition: recursive calls `rel A v`, `rel B (tm_app t v)` inside the match on T ✓ (A and B are subterms of `Ty_Arrow A B`).

Accessors:
```
Lemma rel_lc : forall T t, rel T t -> locally_closed t.
```

Now `rel_cr2`:
```
Lemma rel_cr2 : forall T t t', rel T t -> step t t' -> rel T t'.
Proof.
  induction T as [| A B IHA IHB]; intros t t' Hrel Hstep.
  - (* Bool *) simpl in *. destruct Hrel as (Hlc & Hsn & _). simpl. 
    split; [| split; [| trivial]].
    + eapply step_lc; eauto.
    + apply sn_inv with (t := t); auto.
  - (* Arrow *) simpl in *. destruct Hrel as (Hlc & Hsn & Happ). simpl.
    split; [| split].
    + eapply step_lc; eauto.
    + apply sn_inv with (t := t); auto.
    + intros v Hv Hrelv. apply IHB with (t := tm_app t v).
      * apply Happ; auto.
      * eapply ST_App1; eauto. apply value_lc; auto.
Qed.
```
Note: `ST_App1 : forall t1 t1' t2, t1 --> t1' -> locally_closed t2 -> tm_app t1 t2 --> tm_app t1' t2` ✓. So `eapply ST_App1; eauto` needs `step t t'` ✓ and `locally_closed v` ✓.

Hmm, the `simpl` in the Bool case: goal `rel Ty_Bool t'` → `lc t' /\ sn t' /\ True` ✓.

```
      * (* ST_AppAbs: s = tm_abs ... *) exfalso. eapply non_abs_neq; eauto.
      * (* ST_App1 *) apply rel_sn with (T := B). apply Hred in H1...
```

```
Lemma rel_app_aux : forall A B (s : tm), sn s -> rel (Ty_Arrow A B) s -> forall t, rel A t -> rel B (tm_app s t).
```

Hmm, this is the standard issue: to prove LC of `subst sigma t` we need sigma to be LC on all free variables of t. Our Hsigma only covers variables in dom(Gamma). So we need the lemma: free variables of a well-typed term are in the context's domain.

`Lemma has_type_fv : forall Gamma t T, has_type Gamma t T -> forall x, In x (fv t) -> exists U, Gamma x = Some U.`

Proof by induction on the typing derivation. T_Var: fv (tm_fvar x) = [x] → In x [x] → x = x, Gamma x = Some T ✓. T_Abs: fv (tm_abs T1 t1) = fv t1; the goal: for x ∈ fv t1, exists U, Gamma x = Some U. Use the premise at a fresh y: `y|->T1;Gamma |-- open t1 (fvar y) \in T2`, and IH gives: x ∈ fv (open t1 (fvar y)) → exists U, (y|->T1;Gamma) x = Some U. Then `In x (fv t1) → In x (fv (open t1 (fvar y)))`: is that true? open replaces bvar 0 with fvar y; the free vars of t1 include x plus possibly... fv (open t1 (fvar y)) = fv t1 ∪ {y} if bvar 0 occurs, else fv t1. So In x (fv t1) → In x (fv (open t1 (fvar y))) ✓ (need a lemma: fv t ⊆ fv (open_rec ...)). Then from IH: exists U, (y|->T1;Gamma) x = Some U. If x = y then Gamma x = Gamma y... hmm, wait: x ∈ fv t1 and we chose y fresh from fv t1 ∪ L, so x ≠ y ✓. Then (update Gamma y T1) x = Gamma x ✓ (since x ≠ y). So Gamma x = Some U ✓. 

So we need: `fv_subset_open : forall t k u, incl (fv t) (fv (open_rec k u t))`? Hmm, careful: open_rec replaces bvar k with u, so fv(open_rec k u t) = fv t ∪ fv u (if bvar k occurs) — actually fv t includes all fvars in t; fv (open_rec k u t) includes fv t plus fv u (those in u that replaced bvar k occurrences). So fv t ⊆ fv (open_rec k u t) ✓ (induction on t). Hmm, careful with the abs case: open_rec (S k) u t1 → fv unchanged except... yes ✓.

One more thing to verify: the `rel` Fixpoint with a `match` returning Prop and recursive calls. Let me be careful that Rocq accepts:

```coq
Fixpoint rel (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  locally_closed t /\
  sn t /\
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => True
  | Ty_Arrow A B => forall v, value v -> rel A v -> rel B (tm_app t v)
  end.
```
Guard condition: the recursive calls `rel A v` and `rel B (tm_app t v)` occur in the body within the match on T. Since A and B are variables bound by the pattern of the match on T (the recursive argument), this should be accepted as a nested recursive call on subterms ✓. I believe Rocq accepts this (the "match" on the decreasing argument with recursive calls on the pattern variables is the standard structural recursion).

Hmm, but the recursive calls are inside a `match ... with ... => forall v, value v -> rel A v -> ... end` — under binders. That's fine.

```coq
  intros t t' H. induction H as [x | x y z Hxy Hyz IH].

  - intros Hx. apply IH. apply sn_inv with (t := x); auto.
```

```
Lemma update_eq : forall Gamma x T, update Gamma x T x = Some T.
```

Alright. Now let me think about how the induction principle for `has_type` names things. The constructors:

```
T_Var : forall Gamma x T, Gamma x = Some T -> has_type Gamma (tm_fvar x) T
T_Abs : forall (L : list atom) Gamma T1 T2 t1, (forall x, ~ In x L -> has_type (update Gamma x T1) (open t1 (tm_fvar x)) T2) -> has_type Gamma (tm_abs T1 t1) (Ty_Arrow T1 T2)
T_App : forall Gamma t1 t2 T1 T2, has_type Gamma t1 (Ty_Arrow T1 T2) -> has_type Gamma t2 T1 -> has_type Gamma (tm_app t1 t2) T2
T_True : forall Gamma, has_type Gamma tm_true Ty_Bool
T_False : forall Gamma, has_type Gamma tm_false Ty_Bool
T_If : forall Gamma t1 t2 t3 T, has_type Gamma t1 Ty_Bool -> has_type Gamma t2 T -> has_type Gamma t3 T -> has_type Gamma (tm_if t1 t2 t3) T
```

```coq
(* ============================================================ *)
(* Strong normalization proof for CBV STLC                      *)
(* ============================================================ *)

(** ** Accessibility-based strong normalization *)

Definition sn (t : tm) : Prop := Acc step t.

Lemma sn_inv : forall t t', sn t -> step t t' -> sn t'.
Proof. intros t t' H Hs. exact (Acc_inv H Hs). Qed.

Lemma sn_intro : forall t, (forall t', step t t' -> sn t') -> sn t.
Proof. intros t H. apply Acc_intro. exact H. Qed.

Lemma sn_multi : forall t t', multi step t t' -> sn t -> sn t'.
Proof.
  intros t t' H. induction H as [x | x y z Hxy Hyz IH].
  - intros Hx. exact Hx.
  - intros Hx. apply IH. apply sn_inv with (t := x); auto.
Qed.

(** ** Values *)

Lemma value_no_step : forall v, value v -> forall v', ~ step v v'.
Proof. intros v Hv. induction Hv; intros v' Hstep; inversion Hstep. Qed.

Lemma value_sn : forall v, value v -> sn v.
Proof. intros v Hv. apply sn_intro. intros v' Hs. exfalso. exact (value_no_step v Hv v' Hs). Qed.
```

WHAT? `Acc_intro : (forall y, R y x -> Acc R y) -> Acc R x`. That's the standard Coq definition? Let me recall: Coq's `Acc` is

```coq
Inductive Acc (A : Type) (R : A -> A -> Prop) (x : A) : Prop :=
  Acc_intro : (forall y : A, R y x -> Acc R y) -> Acc R x.
```

Hmm! Yes — actually I now recall Coq's Acc uses `R y x`, i.e., y is a *predecessor* of x. The standard mathematical accessibility is "all predecessors are accessible". The well-founded induction principle then gives: to prove P x for all accessible x, prove P x assuming P y for all y with R y x. So it's induction "upward" along R... 

Hmm, but for well-founded induction with `R` being the "less than" relation, Acc R x means x is accessible from below, and Acc_ind gives induction on the "less than" direction. In our case, `step` is the reduction relation. `Acc step t` with Coq's definition means: all t' with `step t' t` (i.e., all one-step *predecessors/expansions*) are accessible. That's the wrong direction for strong normalization!

Hmm. So `Acc step t` as a definition of SN is wrong with Coq's convention. Instead, we want `Acc (fun x y => step y x) t`, i.e., the inverse relation. Because SN means all reducts are SN: `SN t := forall t', step t t' -> SN t'`.

Let me double check Coq's Acc definition. In Coq.Init.Wf:
```coq
Inductive Acc (A : Type) (R : A -> A -> Prop) (x : A) : Prop :=
    Acc_intro : (forall y : A, R y x -> Acc R y) -> Acc R x.
```
Yes, I'm now fairly confident this is Coq's definition (it's the "accessible from below" convention).

So I should define `sn t := Acc (fun x y => step y x) t`, i.e., `Acc (transp step)`? Hmm, note `transp` in Relation_Definitions: `Definition transp (A : Type) (R : relation A) : relation A := fun x y => R y x.` ✓ (that's in Relations.Relation_Definitions, which is imported!). 

So define `sn t := Acc (transp step) t`. Then:
- `sn_inv : sn t -> step t t' -> sn t'`: from Acc_intro's premise: `forall y, transp step y t -> Acc (transp step) y` = `forall y, step t y -> ...` ✓ — apply to t' with step t t' ✓.
- `Acc_ind` with R = transp step: to prove P x for all x accessible, prove P x assuming `forall y, transp step y x -> P y` = `forall y, step x y -> P y` ✓ — exactly what we need for strong normalizing induction ✓.

So using `transp step` fixes everything. Let me double check `transp` is defined in Relation_Definitions. Yes:
```coq
Definition transp (A : Type) (R : relation A) : relation A := fun x y => R y x.
```
Let me verify.

Also `Acc_inv : Acc R x -> forall y, R y x -> Acc R y` — with R = transp step: from Acc (transp step) t and transp step t' t (= step t t') get Acc (transp step) t' ✓. That works directly ✓.

Let me verify these.

Also I should double check the induction principle behavior in tactics: `induction H` where H : Acc (transp step) t gives the IH `forall y, transp step y t -> P y` = `forall y, step t y -> P y` ✓ — that's exactly what we want (IH for reducts) ✓.


**max_list**: 
```coq
Fixpoint max_list (l : list atom) : nat :=
  match l with
  | nil => 0
  | cons a l' => Nat.max a (max_list l')
  end.

Lemma in_max_list : forall (l : list atom) a, In a l -> a <= max_list l.
Proof.
  intros l. induction l as [| b l IH]; intros a Ha.
  - destruct Ha.
  - simpl in Ha. destruct Ha as [-> | Ha].
    + apply Nat.le_max_l.
    + apply Nat.le_trans with (max_list l); auto. apply Nat.le_max_r.
Qed.

Lemma exists_fresh : forall l : list atom, exists x, ~ In x l.
Proof.
  intros l. exists (S (max_list l)). intro H.
  pose proof (in_max_list l (S (max_list l)) H) as H1.
  apply Nat.lt_irrefl with (S (max_list l)).
  (* H1 : S (max_list l) <= max_list l *)
  lia.
Qed.
```

**fv / subst**:
```coq
Fixpoint fv (t : tm) : list atom :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar _ => nil
  | tm_fvar x => cons x nil
  | tm_app t1 t2 => app (fv t1) (fv t2)
  | tm_abs _ t1 => fv t1
  | tm_true => nil
  | tm_false => nil
  | tm_if t1 t2 t3 => app (fv t1) (app (fv t2) (fv t3))
  end.

Fixpoint subst (sigma : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => sigma x
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (subst sigma t1) (subst sigma t2)
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs T (subst sigma t1)
  | tm_true => tm_true
  | tm_false => tm_false
  | tm_if t1 t2 t3 => tm_if (subst sigma t1) (subst sigma t2) (subst sigma t3)
  end.

Definition cons (x : atom) (v : tm) (sigma : atom -> tm) : atom -> tm :=
  fun y => if Nat.eqb x y then v else sigma y.
```

**subst_open_rec**:

```coq
  - destruct (Nat.eqb k n); auto.
  - auto.
  - rewrite IH1, IH2. auto.
  - rewrite IH1. auto.
  - auto.
  - auto.
  - rewrite IH1, IH2, IH3. auto.
Qed.
```

Since `subst` matches on its term argument which is an `if`, `simpl` will reduce `subst sigma (if b then u else tm_bvar n)` to `if b then subst sigma u else subst sigma (tm_bvar n)`? Rocq's simpl does reduce a match on a stuck `if`? It typically does: `match (if b then u else v) with ... end` reduces by first reducing the if — simpl can distribute in some cases. To be safe:
```
  - destruct (Nat.eqb k n); simpl; auto.
```
i.e., destruct the condition first, then simpl both branches: `if true then u else ...` → u, so `subst sigma u` = `open_rec k (subst sigma u) (subst sigma (tm_bvar n))` → hmm, RHS: `open_rec k (subst sigma u) (subst sigma (tm_bvar n))` = `open_rec k (subst sigma u) (tm_bvar n)` → if eqb k n (true) → subst sigma u ✓. So after destruct with the equation, `simpl` should close it.

```coq
Lemma subst_cons_irrel : forall t x v sigma,
  ~ In x (fv t) -> subst (cons x v sigma) t = subst sigma t.
Proof.
  induction t as [n | y | t1 IH1 t2 IH2 | T t1 IH1 | | | t1 IH1 t2 IH2 t3 IH3];

    + intro Hc; apply H; apply in_app_iff; auto.

  - auto. - auto.
```

When doing `induction H`, Rocq presents each goal with the constructor's arguments. The `as` pattern can bind them. But with indices generalized, the pattern usually binds only the non-recursive/non-parameter args... Hmm. Since `has_type` has no parameters, all args are explicit and generalized. The IH generation: for recursive premises, an IH is generated.

Rocq's `induction H as [p1 p2 ... | ...]` expects patterns matching the constructor's arguments *after* the indices are generalized — actually the pattern corresponds to the arguments as they appear in the constructor type. Let me just try:

Hmm, but the indices (Gamma, t, T) get substituted by these values, so the patterns for the index positions might not be allowed. In Coq, when you do `induction H`, the conclusion indices are generalized and the pattern gives names for all constructor arguments *including* those corresponding to indices. E.g., for `nat`'s le: `le_n : forall n, n <= n` gives a pattern `[n]`. So yes, include them.

```coq
Lemma has_type_fv : forall Gamma t T, has_type Gamma t T ->
  forall x, In x (fv t) -> exists U, Gamma x = Some U.
Proof.
  intros Gamma t T H. induction H.
  - (* T_Var *) simpl. intros x Hx. simpl in Hx. destruct Hx as [-> | []]. exists T. auto.

    destruct (exists_fresh (app L (fv t1))) as [y Hy].

    assert (Hyfv : ~ In y (fv t1)) by (intro Hc; apply Hy; apply in_app_iff; auto).
    pose proof (IHH y HyL x) as [U HU].   (* need In x (fv (open t1 (fvar y))) *)
    - apply incl_fv_open_rec with (k := 0) (u := tm_fvar y) in Hx. exact Hx.
```

```coq
Fixpoint rel (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  locally_closed t /\
  sn t /\
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => True
  | Ty_Arrow A B => forall v, value v -> rel A v -> rel B (tm_app t v)
  end.
```

```coq
Lemma rel_app_aux : forall A B (s : tm), sn s -> rel (Ty_Arrow A B) s ->
  forall t, rel A t -> rel B (tm_app s t).
Proof.
  intros A B s Hsn.
  induction Hsn as [s HstepS IHS].
  intros HrelS t HrelT.
  pose proof (rel_sn _ _ HrelT) as Hsnt.
  revert HrelT.
  induction Hsnt as [t HstepT IHT].
  intros HrelT.
  apply (rel_expand B (tm_app s t)).
  - exact I.
  - simpl. constructor; [apply rel_lc in HrelS | apply rel_lc in HrelT]; auto.
  - (* sn (tm_app s t) *)
    apply sn_intro. intros x Hx. inversion Hx; subst.
    + (* AppAbs *) apply rel_sn with (T := B).

    + (* App1 *) apply rel_sn with (T := B).

      apply IHT; auto. apply rel_cr2 with (t := t); auto.
  - (* all reducts *)
    intros x Hx. inversion Hx; subst.

    + apply IHT; auto. apply rel_cr2 with (t := t); auto.
Qed.
```
Hmm, several issues:
- `induction Hsn as [s HstepS IHS]` — Acc_intro's argument pattern: `Acc_intro : forall x, (forall y, R y x -> Acc R y) -> Acc R x`.
```
Acc_ind : (forall x, (forall y, R y x -> Acc R y) -> (forall y, R y x -> P y) -> P x) -> forall x, Acc R x -> P x
```

So the induction principle has TWO arguments in the case: the Acc-function and an IH-function. So `induction Hsn as [s HstepS IHS]` should match: s (the x), HstepS (the Acc function), IHS (the IH function) ✓.

For `rel_if`:
```coq
Lemma rel_if : forall T (a b c : tm),
  rel Ty_Bool a -> rel T b -> rel T c -> rel T (tm_if a b c).
Proof.
  intros T a b c HrelA HrelB HrelC.
  pose proof (rel_sn _ _ HrelA) as Hsna.
  revert HrelA HrelB HrelC.
  induction Hsna as [a HstepA IHA].
  intros HrelA HrelB HrelC.
  apply (rel_expand T (tm_if a b c)).
  - exact I.
  - simpl. constructor; [apply rel_lc in HrelA | constructor; [apply rel_lc in HrelB | apply rel_lc in HrelC]]; auto.
  - apply sn_intro. intros x Hx. inversion Hx; subst.
    + (* IfTrue *) apply rel_sn with (T := T); auto.
    + (* IfFalse *) apply rel_sn with (T := T); auto.
    + (* If *) apply rel_sn with (T := T). apply IHA; auto. apply rel_cr2 with (t := a); auto.
  - intros x Hx. inversion Hx; subst.
    + auto.
    + auto.
    + apply IHA; auto. apply rel_cr2 with (t := a); auto.
Qed.
```
Hmm, wait: `revert HrelA HrelB HrelC` — b and c are still in the context but that's fine since they're fixed. But the goal after revert: `rel Ty_Bool a -> rel T b -> rel T c -> rel T (tm_if a b c)` where a is the Acc-index ✓. Then `induction Hsna` generalizes a ✓.

But careful: `pose proof (rel_sn _ _ HrelA) as Hsna` — then `revert HrelA HrelB HrelC` doesn't revert Hsna ✓, and induction on Hsna generalizes a in the goal ✓ — but Hsna itself is a hypothesis about a; induction on it is fine ✓.

```coq
Fixpoint rel (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  locally_closed t /\
  sn t /\
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => True
  | Ty_Arrow A B => forall v, value v -> rel A v -> rel B (tm_app t v)
  end.
```

The recursive calls `rel A v` and `rel B (tm_app t v)` are inside the match branch for `Ty_Arrow A B`. The structural decreasing argument is T. Rocq should accept since A and B are subterms of the matched value. I'm fairly confident this works (common pattern).

Let me write the code.

```coq
(** ** Free variables and substitution *)

Fixpoint fv (t : tm) : list atom :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar _ => nil
  | tm_fvar x => cons x nil
  | tm_app t1 t2 => app (fv t1) (fv t2)
  | tm_abs _ t1 => fv t1
  | tm_true => nil
  | tm_false => nil
  | tm_if t1 t2 t3 => app (fv t1) (app (fv t2) (fv t3))
  end.

Fixpoint subst_tm (sigma : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => sigma x
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (subst_tm sigma t1) (subst_tm sigma t2)
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs T (subst_tm sigma t1)
  | tm_true => tm_true
  | tm_false => tm_false
  | tm_if t1 t2 t3 => tm_if (subst_tm sigma t1) (subst_tm sigma t2) (subst_tm sigma t3)
  end.

Definition cons_tm (x : atom) (v : tm) (sigma : atom -> tm) : atom -> tm :=
  fun y => if Nat.eqb x y then v else sigma y.

Lemma subst_open_rec : forall t k u sigma,

  - rewrite IH1, IH2. auto.
  - rewrite IH1. auto.
  - auto.
  - auto.
  - rewrite IH1, IH2, IH3. auto.
Qed.
```

```coq
  - destruct Ha.
  - simpl in Ha. destruct Ha as [-> | Ha].
    + apply Nat.le_max_l.
    + apply Nat.le_trans with (max_list l); auto. apply Nat.le_max_r.
```

```coq
Lemma has_type_fv : forall Gamma t T, has_type Gamma t T ->
  forall x, In x (fv t) -> exists U, Gamma x = Some U.
Proof.
  intros Gamma t T H. induction H.
  - (* T_Var *) intros x Hx. simpl in Hx. destruct Hx as [-> | []]. exists T. auto.
  - (* T_Abs *) intros x Hx. simpl in Hx.
    destruct (exists_fresh (app L (fv t1))) as [y Hy].
    assert (HyL : ~ In y L).
    { intro Hc. apply Hy. apply in_app_iff. left. exact Hc. }
    assert (Hyfv : ~ In y (fv t1)).
    { intro Hc. apply Hy. apply in_app_iff. right. exact Hc. }
    assert (Hiny : In x (fv (open t1 (tm_fvar y)))).
    { apply incl_fv_open_rec with (k := 0) (u := tm_fvar y). exact Hx. }
    destruct (H0 y HyL x Hiny) as [U HU].
    exists U. unfold update in HU.
    destruct (Nat.eqb y x) eqn:E.
    + apply Nat.eqb_eq in E. subst y. exfalso. apply Hyfv. exact Hx.
    + exact HU.
  - (* T_App *) intros x Hx. simpl in Hx. apply in_app_iff in Hx.
    destruct Hx as [Hx | Hx].
    + apply H0. exact Hx.
    + apply H2. exact Hx.
  - (* T_True *) intros x Hx. simpl in Hx. destruct Hx.
  - (* T_False *) intros x Hx. simpl in Hx. destruct Hx.
  - (* T_If *) intros x Hx. simpl in Hx. repeat rewrite in_app_iff in Hx.
    destruct Hx as [Hx | [Hx | Hx]].
    + apply H0. exact Hx.
    + apply H2. exact Hx.
    + apply H4. exact Hx.
Qed.
```

```coq
(** ** The logical relation *)

Definition non_abs (t : tm) : Prop :=
  match t with
  | tm_abs _ _ => False
  | _ => True
  end.

Lemma non_abs_neq : forall s A t0, non_abs s -> s = tm_abs A t0 -> False.
Proof.
  intros s A t0 H Heq. destruct s; simpl in *; try discriminate; tauto.
Qed.

Fixpoint rel (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  locally_closed t /\
  sn t /\
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => True
  | Ty_Arrow A B => forall v, value v -> rel A v -> rel B (tm_app t v)
  end.

Lemma rel_lc : forall T t, rel T t -> locally_closed t.
Proof. intros T. induction T as [| A IHA B IHB]; intros t H; simpl in H; tauto. Qed.

Lemma rel_sn : forall T t, rel T t -> sn t.
Proof. intros T. induction T as [| A IHA B IHB]; intros t H; simpl in H; tauto. Qed.

Lemma rel_app_clause : forall A B t, rel (Ty_Arrow A B) t ->
  forall v, value v -> rel A v -> rel B (tm_app t v).
Proof. intros A B t H. simpl in H. tauto. Qed.

Lemma rel_bool_inv : forall t, rel Ty_Bool t -> locally_closed t /\ sn t.
```

```coq
Theorem fundamental : forall Gamma t T,
  has_type Gamma t T ->
  forall sigma, (forall x U, Gamma x = Some U -> rel U (sigma x)) ->
  rel T (subst_tm sigma t).
Proof.
  intros Gamma t T Hty.
  induction Hty; intros sigma Hsigma.
  - (* T_Var *) simpl. apply Hsigma. exact H.
  - (* T_Abs *)
    simpl.
    assert (Habs : has_type Gamma (tm_abs T1 t1) (Ty_Arrow T1 T2)).
    { eapply T_Abs. exact H. }
    assert (Hlc1 : lc_at 1 t1).
    { apply lc_abs_inv with (A := T1). apply has_type_lc with (Gamma := Gamma) (T := Ty_Arrow T1 T2). exact Habs. }
    assert (HlcS : forall y, In y (fv t1) -> lc_at 0 (sigma y)).
    { intros y Hy. apply rel_lc. apply Hsigma.
      destruct (has_type_fv Gamma (tm_abs T1 t1) (Ty_Arrow T1 T2) Habs y Hy) as [U HU]. exact HU. }
    assert (Hlcsub : locally_closed (tm_abs T1 (subst_tm sigma t1))).
    { simpl. apply lc_at_subst with (k := 1).
      - exact Hlc1.
      - intros y Hy. apply lc_at_weaken with (k := 0); auto. apply HlcS; auto. }
    split; [| split].
    + exact Hlcsub.
    + apply value_sn. apply v_abs. exact Hlcsub.
    + intros v Hv Hrelv.
      assert (Hbody : rel T2 (open (subst_tm sigma t1) v)).
      { destruct (exists_fresh (app L (fv t1))) as [x Hx].
        assert (HxL : ~ In x L).
        { intro Hc. apply Hx. apply in_app_iff. left. exact Hc. }
        assert (Hxfv : ~ In x (fv t1)).
        { intro Hc. apply Hx. apply in_app_iff. right. exact Hc. }
        assert (Hsigma' : forall y U, update Gamma x T1 y = Some U ->
                                     rel U (cons_tm x v sigma y)).

          - apply Nat.eqb_neq in E. rewrite update_neq in Hy by auto. apply Hsigma. exact Hy. }

        { intros y. unfold cons_tm. destruct (Nat.eqb x y) eqn:E.
          - apply Nat.eqb_eq in E. subst y. apply value_lc; auto.
          - apply Hsigma_y? }
```
